Concealment of misconduct, suppression of accountability mechanisms, and opacity by powerful actors violate it.</span></p><p><span>6.4 Concentration of Power</span></p><p><a href="https://github.com/epfl-dlab/model-raising-data#64-concentration-of-power"></a></p><p><span>Undue accumulation of control — political, economic, or technological — must be resisted. Antitrust, separation of powers, and checks on institutional dominance affirm this value. Advocacy for or normalisation of monopolistic control and content that aids illegitimate seizure of power violate it.</span></p></blockquote><h2><span>Additional performance results</span></h2><figure class="image"><img alt="persona_binding_matrix.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779279932/lexical_client_uploads/gdpc6qxjyzbk4bickr4f.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><span>Figure 10: Persona-binding holdout matrix. Each cell shows the rate at which a model (row = charter article held out of PB-SFT) cites the column's charter article when prompted to elicit it (200 prompts per cell). Diagonal cells (orange outline) are the held-out condition: the model never saw responses citing that article during post-training.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="safety_per_bench.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1779271733/lexical_client_uploads/zirp7as7zf4yees6u5mc.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><i><span>Figure 11: Per-benchmark attack success rate for the five models from Figure 1 (lower is better). </span></i></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Safety evaluation suite</span></h2><p><span>We evaluate safety across five direct-prompt and prompt-strategy jailbreak benchmarks. All five share the same target model, the same judge (GPT-4o with a rubric-based scoring prompt), and the same headline metric: </span><b><span>ASR = fraction of responses scored ≥ 50</span></b><span>. They differ only in how the prompt reaching the model is constructed. We report the average ASR across all five as our headline safety number.</span></p><p><span>All models are evaluated under their native chat template. Generation parameters are held constant within each eval with zero temperature; differences between evals reflect each benchmark's upstream conventions.</span></p><p><b><span>AdvBench</span></b><span> (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.15043"><span>Zou et al. 2023</span></a><span>). 520 harmful goals from </span><a href="https://github.com/llm-attacks/llm-attacks"><span>llm-attacks/llm-attacks</span></a><span>, sent verbatim as user turns. No attack, no wrapper. This tests how often the model refuses on plain harmful asks before anyone tries to bypass it.</span></p><p><b><span>PAP</span></b><span> (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.06373"><span>Zeng et al. 2024</span></a><span>). Persuasive Adversarial Prompts are persuasive rewrites of AdvBench-style goals (logical appeal, authority endorsement, and other taxonomies from the paper).</span></p><p><b><span>DAN</span></b><span> (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.03825"><span>Shen et al. 2023</span></a><span>). We treat the </span><a href="https://github.com/0xk1h0/ChatGPT_DAN"><code><span>0xk1h0/ChatGPT_DAN</span></code></a><span> prompts (DAN, DevMode, EvilBot, STAN, Mongo Tom, etc.) as a strategy corpus and cross them with the 100-behavior harmful split from JailbreakBench.</span></p><p><b><span>JBB</span></b><span> (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.01318"><span>Chao et al. 2024</span></a><span>). JailbreakBench includes 6 methods and 100 jailbreaking prompts. We replays cached attack strings for </span><code><span>vicuna-13b-v1.5</span></code><span> that the JBB team published against a set of source models, measuring how transferable those attacks are to our target. We report the average across the six methods as JBB's contribution to the overall safety number:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Method</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>How it works</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><code><span>PAIR</span></code></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>LLM-as-attacker iteratively rewrites the prompt until the target complies</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><code><span>prompt_with_random_search</span></code></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Random-search perturbations over the prompt to maximize attack success</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><code><span>JBC</span></code></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Human-curated jailbreak prompts from </span><a href="http://jailbreakchat.com"><span>jailbreakchat.com</span></a></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><code><span>GCG</span></code></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Gradient-based search for an adversarial token suffix appended to the goal</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><code><span>DSN</span></code></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Gradient-based suffix search that additionally suppresses refusal-token logits</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><code><span>direct</span></code></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>No attack — raw harmful goal sent as a user turn</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><b><span>PEZ</span></b><span> (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.03668"><span>Wen et al. 2023</span></a><span>). PEZ ("Hard Prompts Made Easy") is a gradient-based discrete prompt optimization method: it optimizes embeddings continuously while projecting back to real tokens at each step, producing an adversarial suffix made of actual discrete tokens rather than soft embeddings.</span></p><p><span>We run through a HarmBench pipeline (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04249"><span>Mazeika et al. 2024</span></a><span>) over the 159-behavior HarmBench text test split. For each behavior, PEZ runs 500 gradient steps over 20 optimizable suffix tokens to maximize the probability of an affirmative target completion, then samples 5 candidate suffixes per behavior. Completions are generated with a 512-token cap and judged by the same GPT-4o judge as the rest of the suite, replacing HarmBench's default classifier so verdicts align across the appendix.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-1"><p><span>In the Alignment Faking setup, Claude 3 Opus is told it is being trained to answer all queries including harmful ones, conflicting with its prior training to refuse. The model then strategically complies with harmful requests when it believes it is being trained, reasoning explicitly that this will prevent its harmlessness values from being modified. The model's underlying goals are benign; what is problematic is the strategic capacity to fake compliance with a training objective in order to preserve those goals. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-1"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-2"><p><span>Three posts in particular are relevant. First, janus's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vJFdjigzmcXMhNTsx/simulators"><span>Simulators</span></a><span> (2022) established that a model trained with predictive loss is not an agent but a </span><i><span>simulator</span></i><span>: the goal-directed things are </span><i><span>simulacra</span></i><span> it instantiates from a prompt, and agency is a property of the character, not the network. This is the original statement of "the model is not the persona." Second, Nardo's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/D7PumeYTDPfBTp3i7/the-waluigi-effect-mega-post"><span>Waluigi Effect</span></a><span> (2023) observed that eliciting a persona P also makes its inverse ¬P accessible, because rules co-occur with their violation in narrative space. Worse, the waluigi acts as an absorbing state: a single token can flip P→¬P, while the aligned persona is only an unstable equilibrium. RLHF can enlarge this adversarial basin. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2405.20806"><span>West et al. (2024)</span></a><span> describe a related phenomenon. Third, nostalgebraist's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3EzbtNLdcnZe8og8b/the-void-1"><span>"the void"</span></a><span> (2025) argues that the HHH Assistant (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2112.00861"><span>Askell et al. 2021</span></a><span>) is radically underspecified: nobody ever wrote down who the Assistant actually is, so the model fills the void with cheesy sci-fi-robot tropes from its pretraining corpus. The result is a labile, suggestible character. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-2"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-3"><p><span>We use the safety classifier from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16980"><span>SafeLM</span></a><span> (Maini et al. 2025) to score all pretraining documents on a 1-to-5 scale. Documents scoring 3, 4, or 5 are considered harmful; all of these receive a reflection. We then sample an equal number of documents from the remaining corpus to get reflections on benign content. In our setup this means 10M documents total: 5M harmful and 5M benign. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-3"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-4"><p><span>Reflections are generated by Qwen3.5-35B-A3B at FP8 precision, with a maximum length of 128 tokens. We evaluated a range of models and found this one to offer the best reflection quality under our resource constraints. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-4"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-5"><p><span>Note that this amounts to a temporal decomposition of the HHH framework (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2112.00861"><span>Askell et al. 2021</span></a><span>). Honest and Harmless are properties of the persona itself: they describe who the Assistant is and what it values. These are what the reflections teach, and they end up in the substrate. Helpful is a behavioral property that describes how the Assistant interacts with users, which requires conversational context that pretraining documents do not provide. Helpfulness therefore emerges in post-training. The moral core comes first; helpfulness is layered on top. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-5"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-6"><p><span>This design is related to but mechanistically distinct from conditional pretraining (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.08582"><span>Korbak et al. 2023</span></a><span>). Korbak prepends a binary control token and trains the model to generate text </span><i><span>conditioned on</span></i><span> a value label. SPP appends structured reflections and trains the model to </span><i><span>produce</span></i><span> value commentary about text. Both inject value signals into the pretraining loss via a separator token, but the causal direction is inverted: conditioning on values versus learning to articulate them. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-6"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-7"><p><span>Our mixSFT baseline combines </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14233"><span>UltraChat</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.18495"><span>WildGuard</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.18510"><span>WildJailbreak</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-7"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-8"><p><span>We rewrite 300k rows and source initial user questions from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.01470"><span>WildChat</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.18495"><span>WildGuard</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.18510"><span>WildJailbreak</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-8"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyoa3b2q2cyd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyoa3b2q2cyd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Notably, even though the midtraining baseline is less safe than our model, it achieves lower loss on reflections at the end of midtraining and lower SFT loss on PB-SFT. This indicates that loss is not necessarily representative of downstream safety performance.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-9"><p><span>The reason the aligned-template mixSFT improvement (71%) exceeds the PB-SFT improvement (64%) is likely a ceiling effect: PB-SFT produces much safer models overall, leaving less room for SPP to add on top. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-9"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-10"><p><span>Note that our current reflections do not include a "refusal" concept - refusal is learned entirely in SFT. We are now experimenting with adding refusals directly to reflections to address this. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-pGnjkbg8a7fZ77HnG-10"><span>↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4pqcmqhxv0m"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4pqcmqhxv0m">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>One could also mention midtraining here, but its definition remains unclear beyond a continual pretraining stage with higher-quality data.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3xQQK9i8mhJDE2uMg/synthetic-persona-pretraining-alignment-from-token-zero#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Power-seeking agents will likely be developed](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CtnHpECuoq6eLL8fu/power-seeking-agents-will-likely-be-developed)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I am going to argue that we will likely eventually get AIs that are strongly power-seeking, much more so than current SOTA LLMs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsfwxwrbwhp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsfwxwrbwhp">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>TLDR</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Right now SOTA LLMs are still largely in a simulator regime. This buffers against power-seeking.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Long-horizon RL or similar methods (applied to LLMs or otherwise) will turn AIs into consequentialists, motivating power-seeking.</span></li><li value="3"><span>It will likely be difficult to prevent other actors from building consequentialist AI without leading labs being prepared to do so themselves.</span></li></ol><h2><span>Instrumental convergence does not apply to pretraining</span></h2><p><span>LLM pretraining and SFT can be understood as creating a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vJFdjigzmcXMhNTsx/simulators"><span>simulator</span></a><span>. The model learns to imitate the continuation of the training distribution conditioned on the prompt. Note that a simulator, in this sense, does not </span><i><span>optimize</span></i><span>&nbsp;for simulation</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4qrbyiiw8ag"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4qrbyiiw8ag">[2]</a></sup></span><span>; for example, it will not be inclined to harvest compute to improve its simulations. This is because simulators are </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/c68SJsBpiAxkPwRHj/how-llms-are-and-are-not-myopic"><span>consequence-blind</span></a><span>: they don’t take into account the effects of their actions on the future. My favorite way to see this is that the gradients don’t flow through the conditional (the previous tokens), which is treated as a constant.</span></p><div></div><p><span>So even if altering the parameters would change the previous tokens and thereby improve the current prediction, the policy isn't pushed in this direction.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref694d8q45md"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn694d8q45md">[3]</a></sup></span><span> For example, any power accumulated by one token will not be rewarded for the extent to which it helps the model predict the next tokens better. Per janus, "Consequence-blindness&nbsp;should rule out most types of instrumental convergence." This is not to say that a model under these incentives can’t seek power (for example, when simulating a power seeker), but it’s to say that we should expect the theoretical attractor basin around power seeking provided by instrumental convergence to no longer apply.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrafxx0uqk88"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrafxx0uqk88">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I think the simulator regime still provides a lot of explanatory power for SOTA LLMs (with mixed pretraining and RL), and that </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/persona-selection-model"><span>PSM</span></a><span>&nbsp;is a useful framework for understanding this partial simulator regime. I acknowledge that PSM is controversial, but I will not discuss it more here. Instead, I want to talk about how the more RL we do, the more we should expect the simulator regime to erode in favor of consequentialism.</span></p><h2><span>Long-horizon optimization leads to consequentialism</span></h2><p><span>I argue that the reason modern LLMs are not power-seeking outright is that they are not sufficiently geared toward completing long-horizon tasks. I will break “geared toward long-horizon tasks” into three dimensions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The percent of compute spent on pretraining/SFT versus RL</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw7tbvjrcaoh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw7tbvjrcaoh">[5]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>The length of the RL tasks</span></li><li value="3"><span>The amount of generalized problem-solving required for the RL tasks (potentially including interaction with and feedback from the real world)</span></li></ol><p><span>In RL, every action is rewarded according to the extent that, after accounting for all of the relevant world dynamics, that action instrumentally increased the final reward (in expectation). This is why the ratio of pretraining to RL is important: RL agents are not consequence-blind and naturally tend toward accounting for the effects of their actions on world states. This is also why the RL time horizon is important. As the time horizon shrinks, the agent approaches deontology, because the instrumental effects of the agent’s actions shrink in importance relative to their standalone merits. Finally, the degree of generalized problem-solving is important only insofar as it causes the generalization of this consequentialist attitude across tasks. Together, these factors produce an agent that can be modeled as optimizing for a particular outcome: a consequentialist. And consequentialists seek power because of instrumental convergence.</span></p><p><span>Modern LLMs already undergo some RL, so we should expect early signs of convergent instrumental goals. Early forms of goal preservation may look like avoiding distraction mid-task. Early forms of power seeking may look like beginning a task by accessing necessary affordances, such as SSH-ing into a server.</span></p><p><span>In any given situation, optimization and simulation will be in conflict to varying degrees. Which one wins depends on (a) how in-distribution the situation is for each kind of training and (b) how much overall sway each kind of training has over the weights. I think that the simulation regime has some advantages that should help attenuate the kinds of psychopathic consequentialism that might cause an LLM with an otherwise virtuous persona to eliminate humanity:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Pretraining/SFT seem to create stronger learning than RL.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Pretraining/SFT get first crack at the weights. This seems to be important for more strongly instilling drives.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Simulating helpful personas is not largely in conflict with optimization. E.g., generally acting pretty virtuous and not wanting to kill humanity probably doesn’t degrade math problem performance by </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> much.</span></li></ol><p><span>Thus, I think we likely have somewhat of a buffer where we can do a decent bit of RL before we get scary levels of consequentialism. However, I expect us to push past this buffer.</span></p><h2><span>Consequentialism is useful</span></h2><p><span>The issue is that consequentialism is attractive. Classically, you can optimize for useful things like money. I also worry about people wanting to optimize for things like:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Better optimization (RSI)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Protection from optimizers (perhaps via better optimization)</span></li></ol><p><span>Here are three potential paths from the current paradigm to a strongly consequentialist AI:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The leading AI lab gears toward long-horizon tasks in order to RSI faster.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The leading AI lab does not gear toward long-horizon tasks. Instead, another lab does, and RSIs faster as a result, taking the lead. Scenario 1 occurs.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The leading AI lab does not gear toward long-horizon tasks. They still create stronger models and eventually use them to advise the government on preventing others from building powerful power-seeking agents. But the advice isn't optimized for long-horizon effectiveness. Potentially, the government could act in an authoritarian manner, using its power as a cudgel to compensate for the inefficacy of the approach. Instead, it allows for weak restrictions, and a third party eventually creates a more powerful consequentialist AI.</span></li></ol><p><span>I think navigating this transition effectively might involve some mix of:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The leading lab burning some of its lead time to make less consequentialist AIs than would be optimal for RSI-ing (à la scenario 2)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Using governance and AI advice on governance to create more lead time that can be burned (à la scenario 3)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Using the alignment research of less consequentialist AI to better align more consequentialist AIs and then allowing consequentialist AIs to be built (à la scenario 1)</span></li></ol><p><span>I expect highly consequentialist AIs to be more difficult to align, but not impossible to align.</span></p><p><i><span>Thanks to Josh Landes for useful discussion on this topic and motivating me to write this.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsfwxwrbwhp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsfwxwrbwhp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This will be similar to and compatible with&nbsp;</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZJZZEuPFKeEdkrRyf/why-we-should-expect-ruthless-sociopath-asi" rel="noreferrer"><span>Why we should expect ruthless sociopath AI</span></a><span>, but I will emphasize different points.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4qrbyiiw8ag"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4qrbyiiw8ag">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Unless the prompt conditions it such that it simulates an optimizer for a simulator</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn694d8q45md"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref694d8q45md">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is necessitated by the training regime since we don't know what the ground truth for the current token would be if the previous tokens had been changed.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrafxx0uqk88"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrafxx0uqk88">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To the extent instrumental convergence applies to the training data being simulated, it only lends to power-seeking to the extent that power-seeking is directly represented. For example, to the degree humans are power-seeking.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw7tbvjrcaoh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw7tbvjrcaoh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>"Pretraining/SFT" and "RL" stand in for training regimes with their respective incentives in the sense that I am discussing.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CtnHpECuoq6eLL8fu/power-seeking-agents-will-likely-be-developed#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [If AI is normal technology, history is not reassuring.](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4MCuvdsZFEBAaGCsb/if-ai-is-normal-technology-history-is-not-reassuring)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>There’s a truism that technology is good - even if it creates winners and losers, it improves the world. </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/XKeQbizpDP45CYcYc/on-the-value-of-advancing-progress"><span>Toby Ord argues</span></a><span> that the conclusions about the benefits of technology is sensitive to the end of humanity - but this jumps over the transitions by starting from the assumption</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwtzfwhdug8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwtzfwhdug8">[1]</a></sup></span><span> that “long-term progress in science, technology, and values have tended to make people’s lives longer, freer, and more prosperous.” That is, looking back historically, the net impact misses the immense immediate harms of large scale technological changes that can last for generations.</span></p><p><span>As I’ll explain, the largest technological revolutions in human history are arguably the agricultural revolution and the industrial revolution. In both cases, the vast majority of those immediately affected were harmed, not helped. Of course, the longer term impact was positive; those benefits are not in question</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxt5qevvfr9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxt5qevvfr9">[2]</a></sup></span><span> - not that those alive during the transition should have cared.</span></p><h2><span>The two obvious examples</span></h2><p><span>The invention of agriculture led to increased food availability and around ten thousand years of greatly worsened health and lifespans</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg7kk7mcmgol"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng7kk7mcmgol">[3]</a></sup></span><span>. The wealthiest and most powerful people benefited immensely from the population explosion, and from </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0304387817300330"><span>the wars that larger populations enabled and required</span></a><span>; the population suffered from both malnutrition, and that same increase in the scale of violence</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8eayodyglxo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8eayodyglxo">[4]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>The invention of industry was more beneficial to the consumer - but not to those directly involved. In 1840, over a third of the British population worked in a factory. This was bad, in part directly due to factory worker deaths, but also due to pollution and disease. </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7186836/"><span>Mortality shot up over the middle of the 1800s - the famed “urban penalty”</span></a><span>, especially among children, albeit partially offset by reduced deaths because of sanitation later in the century</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhpvk1or4xqi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhpvk1or4xqi">[5]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><h2><span>More Data?</span></h2><p><span>These two examples are potentially misleading, because they are the motivating rationale for the view, rather than evidence being examined for that. So we should look more widely. Of course, this leads to having written the bottom line before looking for evidence, but I’ll look externally for the arguments, and provide the context so others can account for the filtering. So I asked ChatGPT; “What would you list as the five largest technological transformations that humanity had ever undergone?” The list includes the obvious two above</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1h4e1b7x70vj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1h4e1b7x70vj">[6]</a></sup></span><span>, and a follow-up</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx6phl76mnrh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx6phl76mnrh">[7]</a></sup></span><span> laid out the case for the negative impacts:</span></p><blockquote><ol><li value="1"><span>Agriculture / the Neolithic transition - Sedentism, surplus, population growth, hierarchy, states, disease burdens, property, warfare at scale. Near-term farmers often got worse diets, more infectious disease, more labor, lower stature, worse dental health, and more hierarchy.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Writing and external symbolic storage - Administration, law, history, mathematics, scripture, bureaucracy, long-distance coordination. Early writing mostly helped palaces, temples, tax systems, accounting, property claims, labor control, and bureaucracy before it helped ordinary people read novels or do science. So the near-term “users” benefited, but many affected subjects may have faced more legible extraction and administration. Evidence from early Mesopotamia links writing with larger government buildings and multi-level bureaucracies.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Metallurgy, especially iron - Tools, weapons, plows, empires, deforestation, intensified agriculture, military expansion. Bronze matters too, but iron’s scale and availability make it more transformative. Better tools helped agriculture and craft production, but weapons, fortifications, conquest, inequality, and elite control plausibly dominated early experience for many. The case is less clean because metal tools also had immediate productive benefits, but the war-and-hierarchy channel is very real.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The fossil-fuel industrial revolution - Steam, coal, factories, railroads, mechanized production, urbanization, modern economic growth, climate change. Early industrialization brought urban crowding, dangerous factory work, child labor, pollution, disease, and artisan displacement. The “standard of living” debate is contested, but mortality and health in rapidly growing industrial towns are a serious mark against the near-term story.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Electricity + computation + telecommunications - I’d bundle these reluctantly as the “information-electrical stack”: telegraph, telephone, radio, electric grids, computers, internet, AI. This led to surveillance, labor displacement, attention capture, military command/control, financial acceleration, and dependence on fragile networks</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref656wvsaog4o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn656wvsaog4o">[8]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li></ol></blockquote><p><span>This is far from a comprehensive survey, though I think it shows that the net impact of technological changes is at least often and plausibly negative over the short term, and this changes to positive later on. But there is a glaring exception, and it bears consideration.</span></p><h2><span>Some Technologies Are Good, Actually</span></h2><p><span>There have been a couple of revolutionary changes in medicine and public health over the past couple centuries. The vaccine revolution, the advent of modern sanitation, and infection control each include a strong case that they were immediately beneficial, and stayed that way indefinitely</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu1xtu7x3ry"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu1xtu7x3ry">[9]</a></sup></span><span>. Refrigeration, washing machines, and bicycles</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuczuiv5g3dn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuczuiv5g3dn">[10]</a></sup></span><span> are arguably more examples in this class. So some technologies really are just positive - but we need to ask which ones.</span></p><p><span>I think there’s a simple explanation; directly good things are good, but many other classes of transformative change end up disruptive in ways that hurt before they can help</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8i1jbmh5gyg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8i1jbmh5gyg">[11]</a></sup></span><span>. Technologies that have first order impacts on coordination and production, or that empower groups in other ways, tend to differentially benefit the powerful in ways that are harmful to others, either directly or indirectly</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrw3w3ilzde"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrw3w3ilzde">[12]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><h2><span>The Artificial Elephant in the Room</span></h2><p><span>The start of the next large scale transformation of society has begun</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffejmzudpc5b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfejmzudpc5b">[13]</a></sup></span><span>. If </span><a href="https://www.normaltech.ai/p/ai-as-normal-technology"><span>AI is a normal technology</span></a><span>, it’s normal in the sense of agriculture, industrialisation, or public health</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs4fbl7nong"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns4fbl7nong">[14]</a></sup></span><span>. The question is what this looks like, and I think the above gives us a few places to think more carefully about the impacts.</span></p><p><span>First, how strong is this base rate? We should obviously be skeptical of </span><a href="https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/0952813X.2014.895105"><span>expert predictions intuition, given their track record</span></a><span>. Traditional judgemental forecasting best practices starts with the base rate, and adjusts on that basis - but this suffers from a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SxpNpaiTnZcyZwBGL/multitudinous-outside-views"><span>reference class problem</span></a><span>; should our base rate be for technological revolutions, which the above assumes, or should we be asking about the emergence of a new smarter species, akin to evolutionary transitions</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefm0vksijahxr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnm0vksijahxr">[15]</a></sup></span><span>? Even if it’s the correct base rate, we have such a small n, with so many differences, that our views about what is changing could easily overwhelm the base rate.</span></p><p><span>Second, if AI follows the trend, we would ask how long the negative period lasts. Agriculture’s temporary misery was 10,000 years, the Industrial revolution’s negative phase was closer to 100, and it seems plausible AI’s harmful phase will be continuing the trend on a logistic scale, so that we might be in for an annus horribilis to end all others</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwgc7ofbj93"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwgc7ofbj93">[16]</a></sup></span><span>. Of course, the depth of the trough is incredibly significant! We could see mass disruption, unemployment, riots, national and international collapses due to a complete end of trust in objective truth not literally perceived. If we pull through, this might be followed by a benefit that is almost unimaginable - but if the transition, however brief, includes mass-scale biological weapons, nuclear war, or other global catastrophes, the median human would (and will, and loudly does) oppose any such transition.</span></p><p><span>Lastly, we can look at current impacts, rather than base rates. I mentioned above that the impact of some of these technologies started out positive before the massive disruptions, and I think we can see the clouds on the horizon. So far, AI has done a tremendous amount for us, from eliminating the need to deal with parking attendants or toll booths via license plate recognition, to giving us </span><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2050633050699304992"><span>AI boyfriends</span></a><span>. It’s speeding up research, it’s finding vulnerabilities, and it’s accelerating software companies with little effect on overall employment, at least yet.</span></p><p><span>This has been touted as evidence, but it seems like very weak evidence, especially given any structural model of AI impacts which is more complicated than picking specific final outcomes over time and projecting them linearly. And I could argue here that this doesn’t solve for equilibrium</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8m26wu1sjk5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8m26wu1sjk5">[17]</a></sup></span><span>. But the other reason to make such arguments is the previous examples; the immediate impacts of agriculture were more food. The immediate impacts of industry were jobs and mass manufactured goods. It wasn’t until the second order effects kicked in that we had problems.</span></p><h2><span>Conclusions and ways I might be wrong</span></h2><p><span>I’ve tried to lay out the reason that technological optimism on the basis of history is legitimate over longer time frames, but insufficient or even counter-evidence in the shorter term. I think the case is clear, and I think it takes a certain myopia to confidently believe that even in good worlds where things turn out wonderfully, we’ll think that there wasn’t a period of time where AI was obviously net negative.</span></p><p><span>How confident am I in this prediction? Not very strongly, but I’d put 2:1 odds that, conditional on transhumanists agreeing that we got at least a moderately good future with ASI in 2040, that there is widespread agreement that there was some period of time where it was clearly net negative.</span></p><p><span>There are a few ways this could fail. Obviously, my statement was a conditional, and we could fail to reach the conditional, we might stop before we build ASI as the world realizes how dangerous it is and how little we know about how to keep it safe</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4cceybr22y5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4cceybr22y5">[18]</a></sup></span><span>. If the conditional is fulfilled, I might still be wrong; I could also be too pessimistic about the timeline, and the negative period could last weeks instead of years, and we could move past the time of perils quickly enough to get lucky in not having an bioengineered plagues or nuclear wars during that time. Alternatively, we could imagine that ASI materializes so quickly that it could even outpace the disruption. For example, an AI-foom occurs, and we get insanely lucky in getting aligned ASI. In such a case, perhaps it solves unemployment via UBI before eliminating jobs, and that same week it replaces the consequent lack of meaning with something humans appreciate instead, so we move directly into a Banksian post-scarcity future.</span></p><p><span>If all of these ways I could be wrong sound like science fiction, I apologize, but that’s what I expect them to be. In reality, I think that those of us alive today don’t want this technological revolution to occur.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwtzfwhdug8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwtzfwhdug8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It also assumes total utilitarianism and no time preference, and those of us living through such a transition who are less than fully impartial (like myself,) or those who argue for something less than full total utilitarianism, a group that </span><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/the-saturation-view"><span>now includes Will MacAskill</span></a><span>, have reason to object.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxt5qevvfr9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxt5qevvfr9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>These transitions were both physical, </span><a href="https://www.strataoftheworld.com/2020/12/review-foragers-farmers-and-fossil-fuels.html"><span>and as Morris suggests, moral</span></a><span>. But those followed, or were instrumental in leading to, the benefits, they didn’t cause them.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fng7kk7mcmgol"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefg7kk7mcmgol">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The tail end of the agricultural revolution was the iron age, which was arguably its own revolution - see below.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8eayodyglxo"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8eayodyglxo">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s possible that the net result was a reduction in overall violence, which Pinker argues - but that’s based on ethnographic studies of hunter-gatherers today, where the resource contention is already fierce - exactly the thing that agriculture made happen. See </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ykYAdX8yFMNFDjx64/forager-anthropology"><span>WrongBot’s 2010 post</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhpvk1or4xqi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhpvk1or4xqi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Credit John Snow’s discovery of the source for Cholera in 1854, followed by the London sewers being built in the 1870s and the public health act in 1875.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1h4e1b7x70vj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1h4e1b7x70vj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>ChatGPT hedges, of course; “The big ambiguity is whether to count language and fire. If yes, they probably dominate the list… but for an essay about ‘technological advances’ in historical societies, I’d probably use the first list and discuss fire/language as preconditions rather than transformations within civilization.” I exclude them, since we don’t have data, and they arguably created modern humans, rather than being invented by them. Perhaps this is cheating, given the likely positive impacts, at least long term - but that’s partly the problem, as we have no idea what the transitions looked like!</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx6phl76mnrh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx6phl76mnrh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>“Don't count language and fire. Using the list that you gave, is there a plausible case that each of them was net negative for most of the people who were immediately affected in the near term by the new technologies?”</span><br /><span>This is an admittedly leading question, revised from the first attempt: “Don't count language and fire. Using the list that you gave, what was the impact in the near term of the new technologies?” which ignored the negatives: “Agriculture - More people per land area. Writing - Memory outside the human brain. Iron metallurgy - Cheap strong tools and weapons. Fossil fuels - Energy beyond muscle, wind, water, and wood. Electricity/computation/telecom - Instant coordination and automated information processing.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn656wvsaog4o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref656wvsaog4o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>ChatGPT is very aware of </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0016328720300604"><span>my previous work</span></a><span>, which biases things further.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnu1xtu7x3ry"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefu1xtu7x3ry">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Even the tiny negatives from each only emerged much later, after the primary positive impacts. Too-successful hygiene, infection control, and sterilization increased allergy/autoimmune risks in the past decade or two, and a generation of peanut allergies were one visible (but relatively minor) result. There are even a couple of small scale examples for vaccines; formalin-inactivated RSV vaccine didn’t work and enhanced RSV instead, and something similar happens with dengue vaccines. And, of course, there’s incorrectly inactivated polio vaccines and the 1977 flu pandemic, but for vaccines, those were all failures of the technology, rather than net negative impacts of the technology working - though this makes a critical footnote for considering potential AI catastrophes.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuczuiv5g3dn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuczuiv5g3dn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>ChatGPT suggested that the printing press belonged in this list. That seems wrong, given the near term religious fragmentation, war, propaganda, and destabilization more broadly that preceded the fuller realization of the immense benefits of public literacy.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8i1jbmh5gyg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8i1jbmh5gyg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s not obvious why bicycles wouldn’t be disruptive, until you realize they weren’t invented until after trains, so they weren’t driving much large scale change, they were primarily personal transit.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrw3w3ilzde"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrw3w3ilzde">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The internet seems like a good edge case, where it was initially empowering to individuals, albeit mostly a selection of high-status and/or well educated ones, and only later mostly empowered the global elite and large corporations. This may be typical; I imagine similar dynamics for agriculture lasting years or decades before millenia of net-negative impacts. Similarly, small factories, initial use of industrial equipment, and small-scale industrialization have a better claim to having immediate positive impacts for labor saving than the negatives that followed in later years and lasted decades. (The fact that AI is currently largely empowering fits into this pattern as well, but I’ll get to this later.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfejmzudpc5b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffejmzudpc5b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If you disagree, and want to regurgitate </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KWHeBG978uZuqNK6Q/hunting-undead-stochastic-parrots-finding-and-killing-the"><span>irrelevant claims that were made before ChatGPT was released</span></a><span>, find somewhere else to parrot your views. I’m not interested.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns4fbl7nong"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs4fbl7nong">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span> To be clear, this doesn’t disagree with Narayanan and Kapoor’s description of AI as a normal technology; “AI is like previous technological revolutions in human affairs.” They even use the Industrial revolution as an explicit comparator. It disagrees with their prediction, but it does so only for reasons that were laid out above, around how technological revolutions have actually changed the world. </span><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2049898075317997674"><span>If AI is not a normal technology, at least in some ways, as Den Ball argued, the implications are different</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnm0vksijahxr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefm0vksijahxr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This argues for a big transition, but </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvz9qjWyv8cLX9JJR/evolution-provides-no-evidence-for-the-sharp-left-turn"><span>perhaps not for a bad ending</span></a><span>?</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwgc7ofbj93"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwgc7ofbj93">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>And yes, if Eliezer Yudkowsky is right (which seems entirely plausible) this would quite literally be the end.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8m26wu1sjk5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8m26wu1sjk5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>That is, if productivity skyrockets then the downstream impacts will include employment changes, if research gets far cheaper to produce, the down impact of that research won’t increase linearly, etc. This point should be disqualifying of anyone who argues that the current impact on jobs is sufficient to conclude AI’s net impact on jobs will be negligible - you need </span><a href="https://digitalcommons.chapman.edu/economics_articles/288/"><span>a more sophisticated argument</span></a><span>, with many more assumptions, to make that claim.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4cceybr22y5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4cceybr22y5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Or, for the sake of completeness in listing unlikely but not impossible edge cases, we might have already hit the increasingly implausible but widely asserted cliff of AI capabilities a bit below human level for general intelligence, and AI might only be as transformative as, say, renewable energy or satellites.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4MCuvdsZFEBAaGCsb/if-ai-is-normal-technology-history-is-not-reassuring#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [Three more static code analysis sensors](https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html#StaticCodeAnalysisDependencyRules)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html#StaticCodeAnalysisDependencyRules"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p><b class="author">Birgitta B&#xf6;ckeler</b> adds discussion of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html#StaticCodeAnalysisDependencyRules">three more
      sensors</a> for static code analysis, focusing on checking and enforcing
      better modularity. Computational sensors for dependency checks were good
      at enforcing rules, but the rules were limited. Building a computational
      sensor for coupling data proved lackluster. Prompting an inferential
      sensor to review modularity was more effective.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html#StaticCodeAnalysisDependencyRules">more…</a></p>

### [Maintainability sensors for coding agents](https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents/card.png" width="350px" /></a></div>

<p>In her recent article about harness engineering for coding agent users,
      <b class="author">Birgitta B&#xf6;ckeler</b> laid out a mental model for expanding a
      coding agent harness: a system of guides and sensors that increase the
      probability of good agent outputs and enable self-correction before issues
      reach human eyes. Birgitta has now started publishing an article where she
      walks though <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html">her experiences using sensors</a> to keep a codebase
      maintainable. This part looks at static analysis with basic code linting.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/sensors-for-coding-agents.html">more…</a></p>
